2.2.2 Common Paradigm. The prevalent paradigm in DFL is primaril<sub>y</sub> based on the avera<sub>g</sub>e a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ation mechanism [46]. This <sub>p</sub>rocess can be <sub>g</sub>enerall<sub>y</sub> decom<sub>p</sub>osed into two se<sub>q</sub>uential ste<sub>p</sub>s: 1) local u<sub>p</sub>date and 2) nei<sub>g</sub>hborhood a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ation.

In the local u<sub>p</sub>date ste<sub>p,</sub> in each iteration �<sub>,</sub> ever<sub>y</sub> node $i \in \mathcal { V }$ first <sub>p</sub>erforms a local com<sub>p</sub>utation ste<sub>p</sub> (e.<sub>g</sub>., <sub>g</sub>radient descent) to <sub>up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> it<sub>s</sub> l<sub>oca</sub>l <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>parame</sub>t<sub>ers</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>on</sub> it<sub>s pr</sub>i<sub>va</sub>t<sub>e</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t $\mathcal { D } _ { i } \colon$

$$
\forall i \in \mathcal { V } : x _ { i } ^ { ( t + \frac { 1 } { 2 } ) } = x _ { i } ^ { ( t ) } + \Delta x _ { i } ^ { ( t ) } ,\tag{1}

This communication and mixin<sub>g p</sub>rocess is <sub>g</sub>overned b<sub>y</sub> a wei<sub>g</sub>ht matrix $W ^ { ( t ) } \in \mathbb { Q } ^ { n \times n } \cap$ $[ 0 , 1 ] ^ { n \times n } . ^ { \bar { 1 } }$ <sup>T</sup>o res<sub>p</sub>ect t<sup>h</sup>e networ<sup>k</sup> to<sub>p</sub>o<sup>l</sup>o<sub>gy</sub>, $\mathbf { } W ^ { ( t ) }$ <sub>mus</sub>t b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ong</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ces</sub> $\mathcal { W }$ d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>grap</sub>h’<sub>s</sub> <sub>connec</sub>ti<sub>v</sub>it<sub>y</sub>

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { W } = \left\{ \boldsymbol { W } ^ { ( t ) } \in \mathbb { Q } ^ { n \times n } \mid \boldsymbol { W } _ { i j } ^ { ( t ) } = 0 \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } ( j , i ) \notin \mathcal { E } \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ } i \neq j \right\} . } \end{array}\tag{2}
$$

Then the <sub>g</sub>lobal a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ation ste<sub>p</sub> can be ex<sub>p</sub>ressed in a com<sub>p</sub>act <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>x</sub> f<sub>orm</sub>

$$

## 2.4 Fundamentals of Hidden Subset Sum Problem

The formal definitions of the HSSP and HLCP <sub>p</sub>roblem are

Definition 1 (Hidden Subset Sum Problem [18]). Let $Q$ be a positive integer modulus and $\{ x _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ be a set ofunknown secret integers in $\mathbb { Z } _ { Q } .$ Let ${ \pmb { \alpha } } _ { 1 } , . . . , { \pmb { \alpha } } _ { N } \in \{ 0 , 1 \} ^ { M }$ be $N$ unknown binary vectors. Given � and an observed vector $\pmb { h } = ( h _ { 1 } , \dots , h _ { M } ) \in \mathbb { Z } _ { Q } ^ { M }$ that satisfies:

$$
\pmb { h } \equiv \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } x _ { i } \pmb { \alpha } _ { i } \quad ( \mathrm { m o d } \ Q ) ,
$$

the HSSP is to recover the secret $\{ x _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ and the binary vectors $\{ \pmb { \alpha } _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ up to a permutation ofthe indices $i \in \{ 1 , . . . . , N \}$

Definition 2 (Hidden Linear Combination Problem (HLCP) [18]) Let � be a positive integer modulus and $\{ x _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ be a set of unknown secret integers in $\mathbb { Z } _ { Q }$ . Let $\alpha _ { 1 } , \ldots , \alpha _ { N } \in \{ 0 , 1 , \cdot \cdot \cdot , c \} ^ { M }$ be $N$ unknown vectors for a given $c \in \mathbb { N } ^ { + }$ . Given $Q$ and an observed vector $\pmb { h } = ( h _ { 1 } , \dots , h _ { M } ) \in \mathbb { Z } _ { Q } ^ { M }$ that satisfies:

$$
\pmb { h } \equiv \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } x _ { i } \pmb { \alpha } _ { i } \quad ( \mathrm { m o d } \ Q ) ,
$$

the HLCP is to recover the secret $\{ x _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ and the vectors $\{ \alpha _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } , u p$ to a permutation ofthe indices $i \in \{ 1 , \ldots , N \}$

W<sub>e</sub> f<sub>u</sub>rth<sub>e</sub>r <sub>e</sub>xt<sub>e</sub>nd th<sub>e</sub> HSSP <sub>a</sub>nd HLCP t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e case w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>r<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>secre</sub>t<sub>s are mu</sub>ltidi<sub>mens</sub>i<sub>ona</sub>l<sub>.</sub>

Definition 3 (Multidimensional Hidden Subset Sum Problem (mHSSP) [32]). Let $Q$ be a positive integer modulus and $\{ x _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ be � unknown secret vectors in $\mathbb { Z } _ { Q } ^ { u }$ . Let $\alpha _ { 1 } , \ldots , \alpha _ { N } \in \{ 0 , 1 \} ^ { M }$ be � unknown binary vectors. For each dimension $j \in \left\{ 1 , \ldots , u \right\}$ , let $\pmb { h } _ { j } \in \mathbb { Z } _ { Q } ^ { M }$ be an observed sample vector satisfying:

$$
\pmb { h } _ { j } \equiv \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } x _ { i , j } \pmb { \alpha } _ { i } { \pmod { Q } } ,
$$

where $x _ { i , j }$ is the �-th component of vector $x _ { i } .$ . Given $Q$ and the sample matrix $\tilde { \pmb { H } } = [ \pmb { h } _ { 1 } , \dots , \pmb { h } _ { u } ] \in \mathbb { Z } _ { Q } ^ { M \times u }$ , the goal is to recover the secret

vectors $\{ x _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ and the binary vectors $\{ \pmb { \alpha } _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ , up to a common permutation of the index �.

The problem can be expressed more compactly in matrixform. Let $X = [ \bar { \pmb { x } } _ { 1 } , . . . , \pmb { x } _ { N } ] ^ { \top } \in \bar { \mathbb { Z } } _ { O } ^ { \tilde { N } \times u }$ and $A = [ \bar { \alpha } _ { 1 } , . . . , \alpha _ { N } ] \in \{ \bar { 0 } , 1 \} ^ { M \times N }$ The mHSSP is then characterized by the relation:

$$
H \equiv A X { \pmod { Q } } .
$$

Analogously, the Multidimensional Hidden Linear Combination Problem (mHLCP) follows the same definition, with the exception that the entries of � are drawn from $\left\{ 0 , \ldots , c \right\} f o r$ some $c \in \mathbb { N } ^ { + }$

$$
W ^ { ( t ) } = \left( \begin{array} { l l } { W _ { c c } ^ { ( t ) } } & { W _ { c h } ^ { ( t ) } } \\ { W _ { h c } ^ { ( t ) } } & { W _ { h h } ^ { ( t ) } } \end{array} \right) ,
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\boldsymbol { W _ { c c } ^ { ( t ) } }$ <sub>an</sub>d $W _ { h h } ^ { ( t ) }$ re<sub>p</sub>resent internal wei<sub>g</sub>hts<sub>,</sub> while $W _ { c h } ^ { ( t ) }$ <sub>an</sub>d $W _ { h c } ^ { ( t ) }$ re<sub>p</sub>resent cross-<sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> influences. Let $X _ { c } ^ { ( t ) }$ <sub>an</sub>d $X _ { h } ^ { ( t ) }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>parame</sub>t<sub>ers</sub> h<sub>e</sub>ld b<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e respec</sub>ti<sub>ve no</sub>d<sub>e se</sub>t<sub>s, we summar</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> this structural reduction in the followin<sub>g p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>osition.

Proposition 1. Given the adversarial observation set O, the global aggregation process over $t _ { \mathrm { m a x } }$ iterations can be algebraically decoupled into independent round-wise linear systems. Specifically, for any iteration $t \in \mathcal { T }$ , the privacy inference of honest states reduces to solving the core sub-problem:

$$
\forall t \in \mathcal { T } : W _ { c h } ^ { ( t + 1 ) } X _ { h } ^ { ( t + \frac { 1 } { 2 } ) } = X _ { c } ^ { ( t + 1 ) } - W _ { c c } ^ { ( t + 1 ) } X _ { c } ^ { ( t + \frac { 1 } { 2 } ) } ,\tag{6}
$$

where the right-hand side consists entirely ofinformation accessible to the adversaries. The adversary’s capability difers across iterations. $A t t = 0 ,$ , the adversary can exactly recover the local updates $\Delta x _ { i } ^ { ( 0 ) }$ for all $i \in \mathcal { V } _ { h }$ , due to the globally known initialization. For $t \geq 1 .$ the adversary can recover the intermediate states $X _ { h } ^ { ( t + \frac { 1 } { 2 } ) }$ but cannot determine the corresponding updates $\Delta x _ { i } ^ { ( t ) }$ , as reconstructing $X _ { h } ^ { ( t ) }$ requires access to the internal weights $\boldsymbol { W } _ { h c } ^ { ( t + 1 ) }$ and $\boldsymbol { W } _ { h h } ^ { ( t + 1 ) }$ , which are not observable. Therefore, exact gradient reconstruction is only possible $t = 0 ,$ which is suficient for the downstream attack considered in this work.

Proof<sub>.</sub> S<sub>ee</sub> A<sub>ppe</sub>ndix C<sub>.</sub>

For an undirected graph, red dashed lines denote transparent intra-adversary communication; black dashed lines represent inaccessible intra-honest interactions. Red solid lines highlight trivial links for direct reconstruction, while yellow solid lines identify the core sub-graph targeted for HSSP-based inference. For a directed graph, arrows indicate the direction of information flow. Grey solid arrows represent information flowing from corrupted nodes to honest nodes, providing no utility for inference, and other symbols are consistent with the undirected graph.

## 4.3 Connection to HSSP

F<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>opo</sub>l<sub>og</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>s</sub>i<sub>mp</sub>lifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>process,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>re</sub>d<sub>uces</sub> to a matrix decom<sub>p</sub>osition in<sub>v</sub>erse <sub>p</sub>roblem. In this s<sub>u</sub>bsection<sub>, w</sub>e <sub>es</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bli<sub>s</sub>h <sub>a</sub> f<sub>orma</sub>l <sub>re</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t l<sub>oca</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d <sub>aggrega</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> in DFL can be formulated as solvin<sub>g</sub> a lattice-based cr<sub>yp</sub>to<sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hic <sub>pro</sub>bl<sub>em.</sub>

Theorem 1. Let the continuous linear system $Y = W _ { c h } X _ { h }$ denote the core sub-problem after topological pruning, where $\boldsymbol { Y } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times u }$ is the known adversarial observation matrix, $W _ { c h } \ { \in } \ { \mathbb { Q } } ^ { M \times N }$ is the hidden weight matrix, and $\boldsymbol { X } _ { h } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { N \times u }$ encapsulates the unknown private honest states.

Under the conditions of:

(1) There exists a known scaling integer $\beta \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { + }$ such that $\tilde { W } =$ $\beta W _ { c h } \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { M \times N }$

(2) The honest states can be mapped exactly into the integer domain using a precision factor �, yielding $\tilde { X } _ { h } ~ = ~ 1 0 ^ { Y } X _ { h } ~ \in$ $\mathbb { Z } ^ { N \times u \ 3 }$

(3) The scaled integer weights $\tilde { W } _ { i j }$ are bounded within a known discrete set $C \subset \mathbb { Z }$

Then the recovery ofthe hidden scaled weight matrix �<sup>˜</sup> and state $\tilde { X _ { h } }$ from the aggregated observations can be formulated as an instance of mHSSP $i f C \subseteq \{ 0 , 1 \}$ , or mHLCP $i f C \subseteq \{ 0 , 1 , \ldots , c \}$ for some integer $c \geq 2 .$

Proof<sub>.</sub> S<sub>ee</sub> A<sub>ppe</sub>ndix D<sub>.</sub>

Th<sub>eorem</sub> 1 <sub>es</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bli<sub>s</sub>h<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e a</sub>l<sub>ge</sub>b<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>c</sub> f<sub>ramewor</sub>k <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e a</sub>tt<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>.</sub> H<sub>owever, w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e a</sub>d<sub>versary can un</sub>i<sub>que</sub>l<sub>y recover</sub> th<sub>e groun</sub>d<sub>-</sub> t<sub>ru</sub>th <sub>so</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>resu</sub>lti<sub>ng</sub> <sub>sys</sub>t<sub>em</sub> <sub>cr</sub>iti<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>s</sub> <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> information diversit<sub>y</sub> induced b<sub>y</sub> the network to<sub>p</sub>olo<sub>gy</sub>.

Remark 1 (Topological Asymmetry). The unique identifiability ofthe honest nodes’ private states is governed by the rank ofthe joint observation matrix. Within the minimal core sub-graph, all "trivial" instances have already been resolved and removed; the remaining system consists ofhonest nodes whose states are coupled. A prerequisite for identifying a unique solution is that the resulting system must not be underdetermined. Mathematically, this imposes a structural necessity: the number of independent observations (corrupted nodes)

must be at least equal to the number ofhidden variables (honest nodes) within the core sub-graph, i.e., $| \mathcal { V } _ { c } | \ge | \mathcal { V } _ { h } |$ |.

While this node cardinality is a necessary condition, topological asymmetry is the essential catalyst that ensures the system is not rank-deficient. In a perfectly symmetric network, such as a $f u l l y$ connected graph with uniform weights $( e . g . , [ 6 5 ] )$ , all adversaries share redundant observational vantage points. This causes the rows o $\mathbf { \mathcal { W } } _ { c h }$ to be linearly dependent, resulting in a rank-deficient system $( r a n k = 1 )$ where a unique solution to the HSSP cannot exist regardless of the number of adversaries. Conversely, the heterogeneous neighborhood structures and non-uniform weights inherent in practical DFL break this symmetry. This observational diversity ensures that colluding adversaries capture linearly independent projections of the honest nodes’ states, allowing $W _ { c h }$ to achieve the full column rank necessary for a unique and deterministic reconstruction.

Definition 4 (Lattice). Given a set of � linearly independent vectors $\big \{ b _ { 1 } , \dotsc , b _ { N } \big \}$ in $\mathbb { R } ^ { M }$ (where $M \geq N ) ,$ , the lattice L generated by these vectors is the set:

$$
\mathcal { L } ( b _ { 1 } , . . . , b _ { n } ) = \left\{ \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } x _ { i } b _ { i } : x _ { i } \in \mathbb { Z } , i = 1 , . . . , N \right\} .
$$

The parameter � denotes the rank ofthe lattice, which is also represented as dim(L). When $N = M$ , the lattice is referred to as full-rank.

Definition 5 (Orthogonal Lattice). For an integer lattice $\mathcal { L } \subseteq \mathbb { Z } ^ { M }$ , its orthogonal lattice $\mathcal { L } ^ { \perp }$ is defined by

$$
\mathcal { L } ^ { \perp } : = \{ \pmb { y } \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { M } : \forall \pmb { x } \in \mathcal { L } , \langle \pmb { x } , \pmb { y } \rangle = 0 \} .

Furthermore, the orthogonal lattice modulo $Q$ is defined as

$$

Definition 6 (Shortest Vector and Successive Minima). The length ofthe shortest non-zero vector � in $\mathcal { L }$ is the first minimum, denoted as $\lambda _ { 1 } ( \mathcal { L } ) = \| \pmb { \upsilon } \|$ . More generally, for a lattice ofrank �, the �-th successive minimum $\lambda _ { i } ( \mathcal { L } )$ is the smallest radius � such that the closed ball ofradius � centered at the origin contains at least � linearly independent lattice vectors, for $i \in \{ 1 , 2 , \cdots , N \}$

Figure 2: Topological vulnerability analysis in undirected graphs as a function of the corrupted node ratio �. The total proportion of honest nodes is partitioned into three mutually exclusive categories: (i) nodes directly recoverable via trivial attacks (blue line), (ii) nodes constituting the core subgraph targeted for HSSP-based inference (green line), and (iii) inaccessible nodes that remain hidden within a single iteration (orange line). Note that these three categories are exhaustive, with their sum normalized to 1.

## 7.6 Defense Mechanisms

## B Prior Knowledge

Case 1 is common in decentralized peer-to-peer systems. To implement SA protocols based on pairwise masking [5], nodes often perform a Difie-Hellman (DH) ke<sub>y</sub> a<sub>g</sub>reement [15] or similar handshakes. In a DFL <sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>h, this re<sub>q</sub>uires nodes to obtain network-level identifiers (e.<sub>g</sub>., IP addresses, node IDs, or <sub>p</sub>ublic ke<sub>y</sub>s) of their nei<sub>g</sub>hbors to establish shared secrets. While DH does not re<sub>q</sub>uire ex<sub>p</sub>licit identit disclosure, practical implementations involving identity-bound certificates typically expose the identities of communication partners. Case 2 corres<sub>p</sub>onds to <sub>p</sub>rotocols utilizin<sub>g</sub> anon<sub>y</sub>mizin<sub>g</sub> rela<sub>y</sub>s or secure hardware like Trusted Execution Environments as intermediate a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ators. While these <sub>p</sub>rimitives can obscure the source of individual u<sub>p</sub>dates<sub>,</sub> the reci<sub>p</sub>ient often observes metadata re<sub>g</sub>ardin<sub>g</sub> the incomin<sub>g</sub> encr<sub>yp</sub>ted contributions. For instance, threshold-based Shamir secret-sharin<sub>g</sub> schemes [48] re<sub>q</sub>uire the s<sub>y</sub>stem to verif<sub>y</sub> that a suficient number of <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ares</sub> h<sub>ave</sub> b<sub>een rece</sub>i<sub>ve</sub>d<sub>,</sub> th<sub>ere</sub>b<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub>h<sub>eren</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> l<sub>ea</sub>ki<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n-</sub>d<sub>egree</sub> $d _ { i } ^ { i n }$ . Finally, Case 3 represents an idealized setting where both identities <sub>an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>l <sub>me</sub>t<sub>a</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a are s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> hidd<sub>en, e.g.,</sub> th<sub>roug</sub>h f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y anonymous commun</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on or</sub> bli<sub>n</sub>d h<sub>omomorp</sub>hi<sub>c aggrega</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> I<sub>n</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>case,</sub> th<sub>e a</sub>d<sub>versary on</sub>l<sub>y o</sub>b<sub>serves</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>na</sub>l <sub>aggrega</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> $\bar { \mathbf { \mathbf { \mathbf { x } } _ { i } ^ { ( t + 1 ) } } }$ <sub>w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>re</sub>li<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on a</sub>b<sub>ou</sub>t th<sub>e provenance or</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>i<sub>ze o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>p</sub>art<sup>i</sup>c<sup>i</sup><sub>p</sub>at<sup>i</sup>n<sub>g</sub> set.

## E Other HSSP Attacks

Coron and Gini [10, 11] introduced two alternative methods that reduce the com<sub>p</sub>lexit<sub>y</sub> of the second <sub>p</sub>hase from ex<sub>p</sub>onential to <sub>p</sub>ol<sub>y</sub>nomial ti<sub>me, e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y s</sub>hifti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e overa</sub>ll <sub>compu</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>l b<sub>o</sub>ttl<sub>enec</sub>k t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> $O ( N ^ { 9 } )$ <sub>an</sub>d $O ( N ^ { 7 } ( N + \log c ) ^ { 2 } )$ <sub>comp</sub>l<sub>ex</sub>it<sub>y</sub> <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t <sub>p</sub>h<sub>ase.</sub>

The first method is a multivariate approach, which reformulates the recovery of binary vectors into a system of multivariate quadratic equations. The second method is a statistical approach, which leverages the geometric structure of parallelepipeds and the Nguyen-Regev al<sub>g</sub>orithm [42] to infer the hidden binar<sub>y</sub> matrix throu<sub>g</sub>h distributional anal<sub>y</sub>sis.

## G Experimental Setup

Graph Generation. The main experiments use fixed-edge Erdős-Rényi graphs generated with NetworkX [21]. We construct connected <sub>un</sub>di<sub>rec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>grap</sub>h<sub>s</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>rong</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>connec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d di<sub>rec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>grap</sub>h<sub>s,</sub> <sub>eac</sub>h <sub>parame</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ze</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>num</sub>b<sub>er</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>no</sub>d<sub>es</sub> <sub>�</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>e</sub>d<sub>ges</sub> <sub>�.</sub> A f<sub>rac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>�</sub> <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> total nodes are randomly designated as corrupted nodes. For undirected topologies, the weight matrix � is designed to be doubly stochastic, while for directed to<sub>p</sub>olo<sub>g</sub>ies<sub>,</sub> it is column-stochastic. To ma<sub>p</sub> the wei<sub>g</sub>hts into the inte<sub>g</sub>er domain<sub>,</sub> we scale the matrix b<sub>y</sub> a factor $\beta ,$ <sub>y</sub>ieldin<sub>g</sub> $\tilde { \boldsymbol { W } } = \beta \boldsymbol { W }$

Synthetic Data. For synthetic data evaluations, each node � is assigned a model state $\pmb { x } _ { i } ^ { ( \frac { 1 } { 2 } ) } \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { u }$ <sub>w</sub>ith dimensionalit<sub>y</sub> $u = 1 0 0 \mathrm { ; }$ <sub>, w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> the inte<sub>g</sub>er elements are sam<sub>p</sub>led uniforml<sub>y</sub> from the ran<sub>g</sub>e [0, 100]. To simulate the finite field o<sub>p</sub>erations re<sub>q</sub>uired for lattice attacks, we quantize all floating-point model parameters into integers and perform operations modulo �, where � is a 50-bit pseudo-prime.

Real Dataset and Model Architectures. We evaluate our framework across image, tabular, and text modalities.

• Image Data: We use the CIFAR-10 dataset [1]. The classification model is a Convolutional Neural Network consisting of three convolutional la<sub>y</sub>ers (32, 64, and 128 channels with 3 × 3 kernels) followed b<sub>y</sub> two full<sub>y</sub> connected la<sub>y</sub>ers with 512 and 10 hidden units, res<sub>p</sub>ect<sup>i</sup>ve<sup>l</sup><sub>y</sub>.

• Tabular Data: We use the Purchase dataset [49], which consists of 600 binary features per sample. The model is a multi-layer <sub>p</sub>erce<sub>p</sub>tron with two full<sub>y</sub> connected la<sub>y</sub>ers (256 hidden units). For visualization, the 600-dimensional tabular features are resha<sub>p</sub>ed into a $2 4 \times 2 5$ <sub>g</sub>rid.

• Text Data: We use the Sentiment140 dataset [19] for sentiment analysis, employing a logistic regression model for sentiment <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

Unless otherwise stated<sub>,</sub> each node <sub>p</sub>erforms SGD with a batch size of 1 in the main ex<sub>p</sub>eriments. This settin<sub>g</sub> allows us to isolate the <sub>p</sub>rivac<sub>y</sub> leaka<sub>g</sub>e introduced b<sub>y</sub> SA<sub>,</sub> which constitutes the <sub>p</sub>rimar<sub>y</sub> sta<sub>g</sub>e of our attack: b<sub>y</sub> neutralizin<sub>g</sub> the a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ation barrier<sub>,</sub> the <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>osed <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d <sub>recovers</sub> th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>v</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l <sub>gra</sub>di<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s o</sub>f h<sub>ones</sub>t <sub>no</sub>d<sub>es an</sub>d <sub>ma</sub>k<sub>es</sub> th<sub>em ava</sub>il<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o ex</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> d<sub>owns</sub>t<sub>ream recons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>ec</sub>h<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ques.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> sin<sub>g</sub>le-la<sub>y</sub>er architectures<sub>,</sub> such as lo<sub>g</sub>istic re<sub>g</sub>ression<sub>,</sub> the corres<sub>p</sub>ondin<sub>g</sub> in<sub>p</sub>ut can be reconstructed exactl<sub>y</sub> b<sub>y</sub> takin<sub>g</sub> the ratio between the wei<sub>g</sub>ht and bias <sub>g</sub>radients associated with a <sub>g</sub>iven label. For dee<sub>p</sub> neural networks, we a<sub>pp</sub>l<sub>y</sub> the GIA framework of [16] to reconstruct raw i<sub>mages an</sub>d t<sub>a</sub>b<sub>u</sub>l<sub>ar</sub> f<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>ures</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e recovere</sub>d <sub>gra</sub>di<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> Alth<sub>oug</sub>h d<sub>owns</sub>t<sub>ream gra</sub>di<sub>en</sub>t i<sub>nvers</sub>i<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s no</sub>t th<sub>e pr</sub>i<sub>mary</sub> f<sub>ocus o</sub>f thi<sub>s wor</sub>k<sub>,</sub> we further evaluate the transferabilit<sub>y</sub> of our attack to more <sub>p</sub>ractical reconstruction settin<sub>g</sub>s. ## J Transferability to diferent GIAs

W<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ur</sub>th<sub>er exam</sub>i<sub>ne w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>v</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l <sub>c</sub>li<sub>en</sub>t <sub>up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es recovere</sub>d b<sub>y our a</sub>tt<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>n exp</sub>l<sub>o</sub>it<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e un</sub>d<sub>er</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t GIA <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s an</sub>d larger batchsizes. To this end, we use the unified API provided by the Breaching framework [17] to evaluate several representative GIA im lementations includin [16 59 67]. This evaluation is intended to assess the transferabilit of our structural recover attack to diferent downstream reconstr<sub>u</sub>ction methods<sub>,</sub> rather than to <sub>p</sub>rovide a com<sub>p</sub>rehensive com<sub>p</sub>arison amon<sub>g</sub> GIAs. We consider batchsizes |B<sub>�</sub> | ∈ {1, 2, 4, 8}. For each settin<sub>g</sub>, we com<sub>p</sub>are downstream reconstruction from two in<sub>p</sub>uts: the un<sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>round-truth client u<sub>p</sub>date � and the corresponding update �ˆ recovered by circumventing SA. We restrict the evaluation to trials in which the HSSP-based attack successfull recovers the correct client u date, thereb isolatin the efect of re lacin � with its reconstructed counter art �ˆ. In addition to PSNR and SSIM, we re<sub>p</sub>ort the learned <sub>p</sub>erce<sub>p</sub>tual ima<sub>g</sub>e <sub>p</sub>atch similarit<sub>y</sub> (LPIPS) metric [63] with the AlexNet backbone, for which a lower value indicates <sub>g</sub>reater <sub>p</sub>erce<sub>p</sub>tual similarit<sub>y</sub> between the reconstructed and <sub>g</sub>round-truth ima<sub>g</sub>es.
